← All hearings

Local Law to Prohibit the Operation of Horse Drawn Cabs

Committee on Health

Chair: Lynn C. Schulman
July 15, 2026 · 9hrs 26m · Watch on councilnyc.viebit.com ↗ · View on council.nyc.gov ↗

Summary

Meeting Overview

The NYC Council Committee on Health held a full-day hearing on Introduction 0943-2026, known as "Roman's Law," which would ban horse-drawn carriage operations in New York City by prohibiting new operator licenses after June 1, 2028 and ending all operations after that date. The Bill was named after Roman Mahajan, an 18-year-old tourist from India who died on June 17, 2026 after falling from a runaway carriage in Central Park when the driver stepped away to photograph his family. The Bill is sponsored by CM Christopher Marte and has the backing of Speaker Julie Menin and the Mamdani administration, which supports winding down the industry, though it raised concerns about the adequacy of worker protection language. The hearing ran from 10:00 AM deep into the evening and drew dozens of testifiers — elected officials, animal welfare organizations, equine veterinarians, sanctuary operators, carriage workers, union representatives, and members of the public.

The case for the ban rested on three interlocking arguments. On public safety, supporters documented 96 serious incidents and 40 horse deaths since 1982, noted that the annual incident rate has more than quadrupled, and cited a Columbia University forecast projecting 16 to 30 more incidents and up to 11 more horse deaths by 2030 if the industry continues. Critically, multiple equine veterinarians testified that a horse's prey-animal flight response cannot be eliminated through training or regulation, making the urban environment irreducibly dangerous regardless of rules on paper. CM Marte noted that 128 accidents and 30 horse deaths over 34 years have not produced effective reform, and that after Roman's death, new driver safety protocols were immediately ignored. Former CM Bob Holden observed that his predecessor bill would have taken effect on June 1, 2026 — sixteen days before Roman died — but was blocked for four years by then-Speaker Adams. On animal welfare, veterinarians and sanctuary operators described horses confined to stalls as small as 60 square feet, denied pasture access, found lame and with untreated injuries, and documented an average of 71 horses per year disappearing from the NYC carriage roll with uncertain fates. An independent veterinarian commissioned by DOHMH found that owners refused blood testing and full lameness evaluations, and that two owners did not present their horses at all. On workforce transition, supporters argued the Bill's retraining program, though needing strengthening, offers workers a better deal than an industry with no employer-provided health insurance, no workers' compensation, and a structural dependence on independent contractor status.

Opposition came principally from TWU Local 100, carriage drivers, stable owners, and the equine surgeon who cares for the horses. Union president John Chiarello called the hearing a kangaroo court, argued the industry's safety record compares favorably to other transport modes, and said the workforce transition language in the Bill is vague and inadequate. Industry defenders argued that regulation and enforcement — not elimination — is the right response, and that the ban will strand horses in underfunded sanctuaries or send them to slaughter. Equine surgeon Dr. Gabriel Cook warned that available sanctuary capacity is insufficient and that the industry's failures are almost entirely attributable to regulatory breakdown rather than any inherent incompatibility between horses and city life. Several opposition speakers alleged, without Council rebuttal, that the Bill is driven by real estate developers coveting the stable properties on 52nd and 30th Streets, and one alleged a conflict of interest involving the Speaker's husband. CM James Gennaro was the most vocal Council opponent, pressing the administration for data it could not provide on the spot and arguing that his own reform bill, Int 0937, which includes hitching posts and other safety measures, deserved a fair hearing rather than predetermined dismissal.

Several significant threads were left unresolved. The administration acknowledged it cannot fully account for how many violations have been issued, which horses are working for which drivers, or how many horses have been removed from service — an indictment of the regulatory infrastructure that both sides, in their different ways, cited as central to their case. No formal mechanism was established during the hearing to verify that horses will be placed in permanent accredited sanctuaries rather than sold or auctioned. Friends of Animals proposed that the Bill be amended to create a City fund to buy out horses from willing owners and cover placement fees, which was not acted on. The industry's primary veterinarian was alleged to have been sanctioned seven times by the State Gaming Commission, banned from horse tracks twice, and found guilty of professional misconduct by the State Veterinary Board, without any Council response on the record. No vote was scheduled during the hearing; the record will be compiled and reviewed before the committee proceeds.

Numbers

  • 128 documented horse-drawn carriage accidents over 34 years in New York City.
  • 40 horse deaths between 1982 and 2026 documented by the Manhattan Borough President's office.
  • 8 horse-related incidents in Central Park in the 13 months immediately preceding Roman Mahajan's death.
  • Annual carriage horse incident rate has more than quadrupled over the period studied.
  • When a horse escapes its carriage, a person is injured more than half the time.
  • Columbia University forecast model projects 16 to 30 more incidents and up to 11 more horse deaths by 2030 if the industry continues.
  • 68% of New Yorkers support ending horse-drawn carriages, per multiple polls cited.
  • 68 carriage licenses currently authorized; approximately 52 active carriage owner licenses; just under 160 horses licensed at end of last year.
  • 208 licensed horse-drawn carriage drivers; approximately 107 actively working day-to-day.
  • Average of 71 NYC carriage horses disappeared from the licensing rolls per year over a 7.5-year period, totaling approximately 529 horses.
  • Stalls in which carriage horses are confined are as small as 60 square feet.
  • Horses required to have 5 weeks per year of furlough at a facility with pasture access; examined by a veterinarian every 4 to 8 months.
  • DOT inspects carriages 3 times per year; approximately 1 full-time equivalent health inspector is dedicated to horse carriage oversight.
  • 17 summonses issued for lacking or untimely medical exam records and 12 for missing furlough proof across 2023 to April 2026 — the entire enforcement record for the period.
  • 18 people took the driver training course in 2025; the course costs $25.
  • Industry salary estimated at $65,000 to $70,000 per year; average carriage or license value approximately $70,000; TWU Local 100 represents approximately 150 members in the industry.
  • Approximately $40,000 per horse per year cited by one stable owner as her annual cost of care per animal.
  • Sanctuary placement estimated to cost approximately $6,000 per horse per year; more than 30 nationally recognized sanctuaries have committed to taking retired carriage horses.
  • Cherry Hill fountain loop asphalt replacement due to horse hoof damage estimated at $500,000.
  • New operator licenses would be prohibited after June 1, 2028; all operations banned after that date under Int 0943-2026.
  • June 1, 2026 was the date the predecessor bill would have taken effect — 16 days before Roman Mahajan died on June 17, 2026.
  • Industry veterinarian Dr. Sierra was alleged to have been sanctioned 7 times by the NY State Gaming Commission, banned from horse tracks twice, and received a 2-year suspended license stay from the NY State Board for Veterinary Medicine in 2020.

Action Points

  • CM Marte and the Council to work with the administration to strengthen workforce transition and worker protection language in Int 0943-2026 before it advances, as requested by the NYC Department of Consumer Affairs and Worker Protection.
  • NYC Department of Health and Mental Hygiene to compile and submit to the committee enforcement data it could not provide at the hearing, including violation counts, stable inspection outcomes, horses removed from service, and specifics of the driver training program including language access provisions.
  • NYC Department of Health and Mental Hygiene to resolve pending enforcement review of the driver involved in Roman Mahajan's death for the administrative code violation of leaving a horse unattended.
  • All testifiers whose time expired to submit full written testimony to testimony@council.nyc.gov within 72 hours of the close of the hearing, as directed by Chair Schulman.
  • Friends of Animals to submit proposed amendment language creating a City fund to buy out horses from willing owners and cover sanctuary placement fees for committee consideration.
  • NY State Horse Council to submit its full list of recommendations and disposition language concerns to the committee record for review.
  • NYC Bar Association Animal Cruelty Committee to submit its full written testimony online following the hearing, as directed by Chair Schulman.
  • Coalition for Responsible Carriage Horse Retirement to continue coordinating sanctuary placement agreements, including retaining attorneys to develop lifelong placement contracts, in anticipation of a potential ban.
  • Animal Legal Defense Fund committed to contributing to horse transport costs in the event the Bill passes; to be formalized if legislation advances.
  • Chair Schulman to compile the full hearing record for committee review prior to any vote being scheduled.

Roman Mahajan and his family were visiting New York City from India when they took a horse carriage ride in Central Park. Roman then either jumped or fell from the carriage, suffering a severe head injury. He tragically passed away later that day. To the Mahajan family, there are no words for Roman's loss. When I spoke to you this morning and I heard the pain in your voice, there is nothing we can do to bring him back. But what we can do is honor his legacy, which is what we are doing today at this hearing, to make sure that this type of tragedy never ever happens again. I know this is not easy and we are going to hear from you in a few minutes. Our condolences extend to your whole family.

We know that when a young person is taken from us, cut off in their prime of life as Roman was, with a world of potential and going to college ahead of him, it is — and I say this as a mom — it is really deeply heart-wrenching. When that death was avoidable, where he could be alive if only we had acted sooner, it is a call to action for all people of good conscience, for your family, for all the families and for our City.

We are here to ensure that this never happens again. This issue is not new to our City. It is certainly not new to City government. There are many people who are in these chambers who have been working on this issue for many, many years, but it has never come to fruition. The matter of horse carriages, their maintenance, safety and operation has come up under previous mayors and previous City Councils every year for almost four decades. The time to act is now.

This is not a simple issue because this industry is historically and culturally significant. For years horse carriages have been synonymous with iconic, world-class tourism. The rides they provide have given visitors an opportunity to slow down and take in the sights of our City and they have given hundreds of hard-working immigrant New Yorkers the dignity of a good-paying job. These are drivers who care deeply for their horses and who take the safety of their passengers and pedestrians extremely seriously. I do not take these considerations lightly. Any time there is a possibility of ending an industry, the possibility of job losses for small business owners and employees, we obviously need to think carefully about that. I am a former small business owner myself. I understand the challenges and burdens of operating and working for one. So I am committing here today that we are going to chart a path forward to protect the workers and ensure that they have good-paying jobs moving forward, whether it be in the hospitality or tourism or other industry. We know that you are a vital thread of the fabric of this City and we will not exclude you from this conversation.

I am also very committed to ensuring that the horses involved are treated fairly and compassionately, and we will ensure through this Bill that we will guarantee their safety and well-being as well. So that brings us to the pivotal crossroads where we stand today. As public servants, we must respond to the concerns of our constituents and we must recognize that the appetite towards banning horse carriages has grown significantly over the past four decades. New Yorkers have long been concerned about public safety, with scores of accidents and close calls involving horses, passengers and commuters. Maintaining large and naturally skittish animals in our chaotic City is a dangerous combination and sadly, with Roman's death, we have now reached a turning point.

We have also become a City that is overwhelmingly supportive of the humane treatment of animals, and there have been many incidents involving horses collapsing in the hot, congested environment of our streets. Accordingly, nearly 70 percent of New Yorkers are supportive of phasing out this practice. We want to be clear: the Council's focus is on ending this tradition while protecting the workers whose livelihoods depend on it, and we will ensure strong worker protections in this Bill.

We can and we should have this conversation today with respect and with empathy, no matter where we stand on this issue. I hope and I know that this hearing will maintain a tone of somberness, of sensitivity and civility for everyone who has come here today. I am looking forward to hearing from the administration, from the industry, from advocates and most of all from Roman's family. I am also so incredibly sorry, as I said from the beginning, for your loss.

(00:07:27)

And for those of you that do not know this aspect: Roman, in an act of heroism, tried to protect his mother. Now a law named in his honor will protect countless others — residents, tourists, humans and horses. While there is nothing we can sadly do to change the tragedy of his death, passing his law is one way to seek justice for him and his family, to keep his blessed memory alive and to carry his brave and compassionate spirit forward.

So I now want to turn it over to CM Schulman again. Thank you.

Thank you, Speaker Menin. The circumstances surrounding Roman's death are deeply disturbing. The driver's conduct was reckless and unacceptable and this young man's death was entirely preventable. This recent tragedy comes on the heels of other high-profile incidents in which carriage horses have broken free, collapsed and died. These include Denice, a carriage horse who died after eating a plant toxic to horses, and Ryder, a horse who collapsed in Hell's Kitchen in 2022 and was later euthanized. These incidents have intensified a debate that has been taking place for years in our City about the welfare of carriage horses, the safety of our residents and visitors, the livelihoods of carriage drivers and the future of the horse carriage industry.

Today we are discussing legislation that, if enacted, would wind down the horse carriage industry. Introduction 943 would prohibit the issuance of new horse carriage operator licenses after June 1, 2028 and would also prohibit the operation of horse-drawn cabs after that date. The Bill would also require the humane disposition of former carriage horses, including prohibiting their sale or transfer for slaughter or for use in another horse-drawn carriage business. Finally, the Bill would require the Department of Consumer and

(00:09:16)

Worker Protection and the Mayor's Office of Talent and Workforce Development to develop a job retraining program for former drivers and other workers involved in the industry, with the goal of facilitating the transition to other fields of employment.

This is an industry with a long history in New York City and for generations, carriage drivers have built their livelihoods around this work. But times are changing and we cannot ignore these tragic incidents or the serious public safety concerns they have raised. The landscape of our City has changed dramatically. Central Park and the surrounding streets are filled with bicycles, e-bikes, electric scooters, vehicles and horse-drawn carriages increasingly competing for space. When a horse becomes distressed, collapses or breaks free in this environment, the consequences are devastating not only for the horse but for drivers, passengers and everyone sharing our streets and public spaces. This is fundamentally an issue that greatly affects the public.

These concerns are not based on a single incident. For years, New Yorkers, advocates, elected officials and others have raised questions about animal welfare and public safety within the horse carriage industry. Last month's devastating tragedy has forced us to confront these concerns in a new light and consider whether it is time for a new path forward.

As we consider the future of this industry, we must carefully examine what a responsible transition would require, both for the horses and for the people whose livelihoods depend on this work. We must consider how to protect the horses by placing them in safe and caring homes. We must also recognize the drivers and workers who have devoted years and in some cases generations to this industry, as well as the significant investments they have made in their carriage licenses. We must consider how to provide drivers with meaningful opportunities for new employment. My hope is that we can bring people together around these shared responsibilities.

I know emotions are running high today on all sides of this issue and I want to conclude by reminding everyone that although there may be strong opinions on this legislation and on the future of this industry, we must maintain a civil and respectful proceeding. The purpose of this hearing is to ensure that all voices and all perspectives can be heard today. We must maintain decorum throughout the proceedings.

(00:12:23)

Good morning, everyone. I would like to give a huge thank you to Speaker Menin and CM Schulman for holding today's historic hearing and to the thousands of advocates and everyday New Yorkers who for years have been demanding we take action on horse carriages in Central Park. For decades we have seen carriage horses live in cramped stables. We have seen them march through midtown traffic breathing in exhaust. We have seen them pull thousands of pounds in the heat and in the cold. We have seen them get scared, run off and die in the streets. Every time an incident occurs, we are given an excuse. We are told this is just an isolated incident and the industry is completely safe. But the reality is that in the past 34 years there have been 128 documented horse carriage accidents. Thirty horses have died. Dozens of horses and people have been injured and hospitalized. Vehicles have been damaged and destroyed and most recently we lost the life of a young tourist from India.

As legislators, now is the time for serious accountability. While the City Council has dragged its feet through endless debates, we now have the loss of a boy on our hands. These are not unrelated accidents but a pattern of failure that proves this industry does not work in modern-day New York City. During this hearing, we are going to hear a lot more excuses. We will be told that the industry is safe yet somehow also needs safety reforms. That if the drivers are just trained better, this will never happen again. That if we just add additional infrastructure to Central Park, such as hitching posts, that will solve the problem. We will be told that the issue is just the lack of government oversight and we will be threatened that if we pass this Bill all the horses will be sent to the slaughterhouse.

However, these excuses do not hold up. The Department of Health tried to provide oversight over the carriage industry. But when the agency ordered independent veterinarians to inspect the stables and the horses, the workers refused to allow them to test their horses. That veterinarian entered the area and found horses stuck in stables too small, covered in scars and exhibiting signs of many medical issues that could disqualify them from working. It was not the City who did not enforce. It was the industry that refused to comply.

After 128 horse carriage accidents, it was only when someone was killed that additional training was provided to the drivers. Legislation has never been required to mandate better training. Either the owners have ignored training their drivers or the training has not been working. For days after Roman's death, we were promised that drivers were going to be trained to never leave their horses unattended. Drivers were documented doing exactly that.

No amount of driver training would change the nature of horses. They can and will get scared and run off. Even hitching posts, which we are told are the magic bullet to stop horse accidents, are not a real solution. A 1,500-pound horse will still get scared while tied to a hitching post. Their halters can snap under extreme pressure and the horses can run off. They can even pull the post onto the ground as the horse pulls back violently. They can fall with the carriage and capsize. And even if the harness does not snap, the extreme pressure on the top of the head can cause serious and lasting injuries. It can even break their neck.

Lastly, we must put to rest the idea that banning horse carriages will lead to the death of horses. We are going to hear today from a broad coalition of sanctuaries and farms willing to pay owners full prices for their horses. Sending the horses to the slaughterhouse is completely unnecessary. If we all work together, we can pass this Bill, compensate the owners and let the horses live their lives on open pastures. The preventable death of Roman has proven that we cannot reform our way out of this. But there is a way forward and that is Roman's Law, Int 0943-2026. With this Bill, we can end this industry, find safe homes for the horses and transition workers to other jobs. My office has already been in discussion with developers of proposals for workforce development programs for workers. Roman's Law is how we can truly honor Roman so we can offer his family more than just words but action. We can protect the horses, take care of the workers and make sure that this tragedy never happens again, ever. I look forward to today's discussion and urge my colleagues to sponsor this legislation so we can finally put an end to this form of legalized animal cruelty.

Good morning, members of the committee, and thank you for letting us speak. My name is Sylvia Tukral. I am here along with my husband Onrawl. (00:37:29)

Good morning, Madam Speaker, Chair Schulman, members of the Committee on Health and other Council members. I am Karen Shift, Deputy Commissioner for Environmental Health at the New York City Health Department, on behalf of Commissioner Martin. Thank you for the opportunity to testify. Also present is the Deputy Commissioner of External Affairs at the New York City Department of Consumer and Worker Protection. Before discussing the legislation, I want to express my deep condolences, and those of the Health Commissioner and all of us at the Health Department, to the family of Romance Mahajan. His death associated with a horse carriage ride was a terrible tragedy. It is unimaginable what his family is going through.

(00:38:31)

The oversight of the carriage horse industry requires citywide coordination, and the Health Department works closely with many other agencies also involved, including NYPD, DOT and New York City Parks. We also work closely with the Mayor's Office of Animal Welfare. The Health Department is charged with issuing initial and renewal licenses for the carriage horses under the Administrative Code. Our process includes reviewing the horse's health certificates to check that the horses are examined by their veterinarian every four to eight months, as well as reviewing owner records showing that the horses have had at least five weeks a year of vacation or furlough at a horse stable that allows daily access to a paddock or pasture. We make periodic observations of the horses' movement and overall body...

(00:39:18)

...condition at the three Central Park hack stands where carriages must pick up and discharge passengers, and we monitor whether to issue related suspensions. We also conduct the training course for aspiring carriage horse drivers, which is a prerequisite to obtain a horse carriage driver's license.

(00:39:40)

Mayor Mamdani has been clear that New York City should join the other cities that no longer allow operation of a horse carriage industry, and the administration supports the intent of Int 0943-2026 to promote a good outcome for the horses. The Bill would require the Health Department to provide horse owners with information about individuals and organizations that can receive or acquire horses for humane purposes, and require owners to certify that they are not transferring or selling their horse for an inhumane purpose. The Bill provides a two-year phaseout period, providing time for owners and drivers to make this transition, and requires a workforce development program. The administration is, however, critical of the insufficiency of the worker protections in the Bill, and we look forward to working with the Council to address those concerns as we move forward on this important issue for New Yorkers. Thank you for the opportunity to testify. Deputy Commissioner Ortiz and I can now take your questions.

(02:03:24)

Good morning, Chair Schulman, members of the Committee. Thank you for the opportunity to testify today. I represent New York State Senate District 47, which includes Hell's Kitchen, where the horse carriage stables are located, and parts of Central Park where these carriages operate every day. Every day, I and my colleagues who also represent this district — CM Carl Wilson and CM Gale Brewer — we witness 1,500 pound animals pulling carriages nose to tailpipe through Manhattan gridlock, behind buses, trucks and taxis, through construction sites. I have watched this issue unfold firsthand. Our district has been the site of multiple horse carriage deaths, including Ryder and Lady. After years of seeing these tragedies repeat themselves, I have reached one unavoidable conclusion: the status quo is not sustainable.

And then, of course, we witnessed what so many of us feared would happen — the loss of human life. Romance came to New York to experience the world's greatest City. Instead, his family experienced the most unimaginable tragedy. His death made clear what many of us have known for years, what the Central Park Conservancy has been saying: that tourist horse carriages are fundamentally incompatible with modern Midtown Manhattan.

I want to make a very important point that this Bill is the best path forward for the workers. Now is the time to help them transition before the industry's decline leaves them with fewer options. Waiting only makes this transition harder as we move forward. The carriage drivers must be part of this process. They must be kept informed every step of the way and have a seat at the table, and be given access to new employment opportunities with the wages, benefits, health care and job security that they do not currently have.

Finally, I want to say that it is so important that we do not demonize each other during this process. That includes the drivers, the stable owners, the union, the animal welfare advocates and the elected officials. I want to thank my former colleagues in the City Council for supporting this legislation. I know firsthand that this is not an easy issue. Not every horse accepts being tethered. They can feel tension, panic and back up and damage what is behind them. Horses can rub their bridles off on hitching posts, creating a whole different set of problems. So please pass Romance's Law. No more tragedies. Thank you.

(02:40:14)

Thank you. Good afternoon, Chair Kalman, members of the Committee, Council, and the sponsor of this law. Thank you, Speaker Julie Menin, for your support of this legislation and for holding this hearing and being here personally. It means a lot. I am Brad Hoylman-Sigal. I had the honor of serving New Yorkers as Manhattan Borough President. You know, Times Square was once lined with blacksmiths and stables and home to the Vanderbilt American Horse Exchange. In fact, my extended family once ran a small business called Miller's Harnesses. Even though Miller's Harnesses is no longer in business and there have been dramatic changes in the transport of goods, services and people in Manhattan, as we all know, the horse carriage industry continues to operate on the busiest pathways of Central Park, the world's third most visited tourist attraction.

Today we are reckoning not only with an industry many say needs to be more tightly regulated, if not outlawed altogether, but with the death of a young man, Munch Baha John, who came to New York for what was supposed to be the trip of a lifetime and who fairly assumed, along with his family, that when they took a City-regulated tourist ride, it would be safe. That was an avoidable tragedy. One no family should ever have to face. The death of anyone, especially a young person, should be enough to demand change.

But for my testimony today, my team and I wanted to take a step back and look at some sober statistics around the issue. So we reviewed public records, including the Department of Health and Mental Hygiene data, for every documented report of carriage horse incidents in and around Central Park dating back to 1982. What we found was evidence of 96 serious carriage horse incidents, including human injuries, and 40 horse deaths between 1982 and 2026. The annual incident rate has more than quadrupled, and in just under half those cases a horse died. When a horse escapes its carriage, our data showed that a person is hurt over half the time. The trend is only getting worse. Over the last 13 months alone, we counted eight incidents, more than double the pace the industry has averaged since 2019.

We collaborated with a professor from the mathematics department at Columbia University on a forecast model based on this data set. If the pattern holds, our forecast shows we could see 16 to 30 more carriage incidents by 2030, almost certainly with human injuries, and upwards of 11 more horse deaths. New Yorkers are concerned about this trend line too. We have seen recent polling data that shows 68% of respondents supported ending horse-drawn carriages in New York City. In concert with this finding, 311 data shows 175 horse-related animal abuse complaints since 2022, a third of them from inside two streets of the park in the industry's own stables.

It is clear the status quo is unacceptable. That is why I joined my colleagues in co-sponsoring this law. But any serious case for winding this industry down has to reckon with the hundreds of workers who would be at risk of losing their livelihood. I want to be clear that the drivers doing this job every day are not to blame for these tragedies, and the City has an

(02:43:41)

obligation to collaborate with them on finding a professional landing. Int 0943-2026 directs to build a workforce transition program. I urge this Committee to make sure it is funded and staffed before drivers lose their routes. Int 0943-2026 also prohibits selling or transferring any horses for slaughter or into other City carriage businesses. Last September, two dozen equine rescues and sanctuaries offered to place every horse this industry retires. I hope this Council, and I know this Council, will work with that coalition to ensure a smooth and safe transition. The full horse incident record review of our 301 data and our analysis is available online in our report, "Should New York City Ban Horse Carriages?" In tribute to his loving family, who I understand are with us today, I urge the Council to pass Roman's Law. Thank you very much.

(02:44:44)

Thank you very much. Sullivan. If you can keep it brief, we would appreciate it.

(02:44:52)

Absolutely. Good afternoon. My name is Erica Sullivan. I am here from New York State Assembly Member Deborah Glick's office to read her testimony. Thank you for the opportunity to testify in strong support of the passage of Int 0943-2026 to ban the operation of horse-drawn carriages in New York City, ensure the drivers a transition to employment and retire the horses to sanctuaries for their healing and freedom.

Horse-drawn carriages are an outdated industry where animals are exploited for profit as entertainment, comparable to the use of wild animals in circuses, which the City has outlawed. The incidents involving horse-drawn carriages are predictable results of horses being put to work in our City and represent preventable chaos and tragedy. We learned the news of horses fleeing and colliding with cars resulting in injury, collapsing and taking their last breaths on our streets, and the tragic death of Roman Mahajan. To his family and friends, I extend my sincere condolences.

Horses should not be subjected to breathing in exhaust fumes, dressed in costume with blinders on and living in buildings that are ill-suited to comfortably shelter them, even on our City's most beautiful day. This attraction is antiquated. Fifteen years ago we were having the same discussion, with calls for replacing horse-drawn cabs with electric vehicles to carry visitors through our parks. It is time for this debate to finally end. Horses bolt when frightened and collapse when overworked, and no amount of tighter reins, hitching posts, studies, regulation or reform could effectively avoid or alter the innate behavioral responses of these powerful animals. The only way to avoid the next tragedy involving horses is to pass Int 0943-2026 and ban horse-drawn carriages in New York City. Thank you for your time and attention.

Taking a horse and carriage ride in Central Park is one of the most iconic things to do in New York City and people from all over the world know about us and travel to New York to come for a carriage ride in the park. A ride in the park is folklore.

In the past 20 years there has been a concerted effort to drive us off our land on the West Side and out of our buildings in Manhattan, and this coincides with the rezoning of the area now referred to as the Hudson Yards, where all of our stables are located. This intimidation has taken various forms over the years, between reporting us to ICE and the keyboard warriors, traction also on social media, and animal rights volunteers who have come to Central Park to harass our drivers and the business in general and create an unsafe environment in Central Park. It is no wonder that their actions have caused incidents and accidents over the years. On more than one occasion, some of these people — the people who testified here before you today — have been arrested for attacking and assaulting carriage drivers and our customers. In Ireland, the British planted yew trees and shrubs on the land that they stole from us.

(02:53:38)

It is good to be here in the same room with all of you in person and not online in some comments section. Horses helped build this very building 200 years ago. There is George Washington and his horse, Old Blue. Unlike the last panel that purports to speak for us, in the rest of this hearing you are going to hear from our drivers and carriage owners, and you can hear from them about how much they love their horses. Who are other people to speak for our horses than the people that know them best?

Horse-drawn cabs have been licensed and regulated. Rates have been set by the City since 1813 and we have been giving rides in Central Park since the day the park opened. That is the Rental Horse Licensing and Protection Law, in effect since 1981. We are the definition of a lawful, regulated business and our safety statistics bear it out. If anything has changed since the Borough President's testimony in the last 40 years, it is smartphones. People are disconnected from reality but at the same time they can post things they see online all the time.

History has shown us that absolutely horrible things have been done in the name of progress and many beautiful things are destroyed. Opponents of the carriage industry have weaponized push polls to make everybody say the majority of New Yorkers want this ban. That has not been borne out by our union's polls of Central Park users. Seventy percent of Central Park users found that carriage horses add beauty and charm to the park and 60% oppose the ban when this was done last fall. My real-world experience bears this out — the smiles you see from people in Hell's Kitchen, the delight in children petting horses in the park.

Real-world experience: we were closed for six months during the pandemic. We went into the pandemic with 68 carriages and four stables and we came out with 68 carriages and four stables. Think of all the New York City businesses that we lost, and now you are here trying to lose another one.

There has been a lot of talk about job retraining and workforce development. I am a horse person. I grew up in Lexington, Kentucky, horse capital of the world. I own a horse. His name is Sherman. I expect he is 12 years old and I expect him to live for another 20 years. This Bill is telling me that I cannot go work with my horse in another place to pull a horse and carriage. I would wonder then if I could have my horses as rental riding horses in Central Park. The Borough President just said his family is from Miller's Harness Company, Miller's Horse Company from Manhattan, which started the mounted unit. Could I get a job with them in the stable or riding as a police officer? Those horses are here in Midtown in traffic in the streets. They are owned by the City, but that is a good City job, and that is what I am skilled at. I do not need to be told to go work in a hotel or something like that. I am a horse person. So will the City be giving me a job with one of your police horses?

(02:59:14)

Dr. Cook? Yes. Can you hear me? Yeah. Good afternoon. But first I...

(02:59:20)

I am so sorry for the tragic tragedy that brings us all here today. My name is Gabriel Cook. I am a board-certified equine surgeon and I provide medical care to many of the New York City carriage horses. Four years ago, members of the industry reached out to me and asked if I would be willing to offer more consistent medical care. I agreed with the understanding that my examinations would be more critical in nature, complete physical examinations would always be performed and horses would be trotted during the examinations to ensure soundness. If found unfit in any way, the horse would be temporarily suspended until healthy.

I also provide tele-consultations when needed and made my referral hospital available to them 24 hours a day. I made it clear that I would be an advocate for the horse and they accepted wholeheartedly. I will not agree with the other panel that horses should not work, and we can get into that later if you would like. Having been doing this for the past four years, let me tell you what I do know. These horses are not abused. They are not neglected. They are not starved. They are not overworked. They are provided proper stabling, clean stalls, good ventilation and as much hay, grain and water as they desire. Stable staff attend these horses 24 hours a day, a standard beyond what I am used to.

Let me tell you what I also know. Despite the intention of the Council's Bill to guarantee the welfare of these horses, it is not possible. There are already innumerable horses unsuccessfully seeking homes. I work with sanctuaries. I work with rescues. They are underfunded, they struggle and sometimes they overburden themselves with horses and the horses do not get the proper care. I am in the trenches. I see these horses. I do this seven days a week. I will tell you this: they will, and when they do become displaced, they will end up in slaughterhouses. I have seen it. They will end up in auctions. They will be euthanized because people do not want to starve these horses. This is not okay, folks, and it is not right.

Finally, having worked in this industry for the past four years, I am in agreement that the industry has its challenges and almost all of them relate to failure in regulation and enforcement. As you so kindly predicted, sir, we would be talking about regulation and enforcement. Almost all of my frustration involves this issue. I have had frustrations resolving this issue. It is extremely disappointing that no New York City agency or the New York City government has been willing to take control and responsibility. I truly, truly believe that with the proper regulation, enforcement and collaboration — and here is the word...

(03:02:07)

(03:18:32)

So there are drivers who will not work with certain owners because they do not like the percentage. There are drivers who will not work with a certain carriage because they do not like the horse. There are carriage drivers who — I mean, when we go on vacation, we decide when we go. We are truly independent contractors. There are some W-2 employees that work for multi-carriage owners...

(03:19:48)

(03:20:39)

The drivers always get the tips, whoever is doing the driving — the owner or the driver. Is it 50%? Does the driver get to keep the first ride of the day? These are little small businesses. So it is not a wage job. Today, if we were not here, we would have been suspended because of the heat. So it is not a salary. This is not an hourly wage job. This is a way of life with horses and small businesses. In this case the workers own the means of production. I own a horse. I work for somebody who owns a carriage. I run his small family business. He has horses, he owns the medallion. There are stable owners here — they own the stables, but they are also workers. We own the horses, we own the carriages, we own the medallions, we own the stables, and that is who we are. Thank you.

Okay, great. Thank you. I would like to thank the City Council for considering the welfare and safety of New York City's carriage horses. My name is Dr. Craig Kulikovsky. I am a New Yorker with 30 years of experience and I have been a horseman for more than 40 years. Before I was a veterinarian, I competed

(03:44:07)

internationally as an equestrian in several countries and on multiple continents. I believe that horses, like humans, can work for a living. But when humans employ horses for entertainment or profit, we assume a serious responsibility to protect their health and welfare. In many equine industries, including racing and competitive sport, working horses undergo consistent, thorough and independent veterinary examinations. These evaluations include observing the horse at the trot to identify lameness, along with blood testing for painkillers, sedatives, stimulants and other medications that could mask an underlying disease or lameness.

When I was commissioned by the Department of Health to conduct an examination of 16 carriage horses last year, the owners did not comply with two critical components of typical examinations: blood testing and the complete lameness evaluation. In fact, two owners did not even show up to present their horses for examination. In spite of the refusal to comply fully, the truncated exams that I did conduct revealed some troubling concerns for me, including widespread old scars on horses' backs from ill-fitting gear, insufficient dental care and lameness that could be visible even at the walk.

While these clinical findings gave me concerns, it was the lack of natural access to pasture that was my biggest concern. A horse that has consistent access to natural pasture is not only going to be a healthier horse, but most likely less apt to frantically grab for unnatural, toxic forage in Central Park, which may have contributed to at least one recent horse death in the City. Not having access to consistent pasture also means these horses spend the majority of their days indoors, inside horse stalls. Please

(03:45:56)

bear in mind... can you please summarize? You can always submit testimony. We have another 10 hours of testimony. I just want people to know, based on who signed up, we have to keep it short.

(03:46:13)

Done in just a second. Okay. Please bear in mind that a typical 12-foot by 12-foot horse stall for a thousand-pound horse would be similar to a 200-pound human living in a five-foot by six-foot closet for the majority of the day. It is not hard to imagine how this type of daily confinement might lead to unpredictable and extremely dangerous actions by a thousand-pound animal when it finally gets outside of its confinement. The concern is not only that one cannot evaluate the horse's welfare without full cooperation from owners, but how do we provide adequate relief from prolonged confinement and the dangers associated with cooped-up and pent-up horses. Thank you for your consideration.

(03:48:24)

The Conservancy supports the Roman Law and we urge the Council to pass it without delay. We do not take this position lightly. Over the past 13 months, Central Park has seen eight separate horse-related incidents: runaway carriages, collisions, injured drivers and a horse that collapsed and died in front of stunned parkgoers last month. That pattern ended in tragedy. An 18-year-old named Roman, visiting with his family from India, was killed when a horse bolted near Cherry Hill. It was a devastating loss and our hearts go out to this family, who have spoken so eloquently here today.

Public safety is the Conservancy's paramount concern. Horse-drawn carriages are simply incompatible with a modern, crowded park used by millions of visitors a year. This has become painfully clear over the last 13 months, during which there have been eight frightening incidents, sadly causing the death of Roman. The park is not built for the volume of people we welcome every day to share safely with half-ton animals whose natural instinct is to bolt when spooked. No amount of regulation and no single piece of hardware can correct for an animal's basic instincts.

The public agrees. A poll the Conservancy commissioned two weeks ago found that 68% of New Yorkers support ending City licensing of horse-drawn carriages in the park, support that holds across every borough, every demographic and political affiliation. Keeping New Yorkers safe does not and must not come at the expense of those affected. We strongly support the provision of real jobs and workforce development services for the drivers and stable workers who will be displaced, as well as good homes for the horses. We would not be the first to do so.

(03:50:17)

London, Salt Lake City, Philadelphia already have shown how to end this practice responsibly, and it is time for New York City to join them. Who is next?

(03:50:31)

Good afternoon. My name is Dr. Natalie Juris and I am an equine veterinarian. My role is to evaluate equine health and soundness, diagnose disease and injury and provide recommendations that prioritize both equine welfare and public safety. I appreciate the opportunity to offer a veterinary perspective on this issue.

One of the most fundamental principles of medicine is that horses are prey animals. Even healthy, highly trained horses retain an innate flight response, a biological survival mechanism that cannot be eliminated through training, experience or routine veterinary care. As veterinarians, we can determine whether a horse is medically fit for work at the time of an examination, but we cannot predict or guarantee that a horse will respond to unexpected stimuli in a complex and rapidly changing environment.

As veterinarians, we also recognize that animal welfare extends beyond the absence of disease or lameness. Welfare includes an animal's physical health, behavioral needs and its ability to cope with the environment in which it lives and works. A veterinary examination is a snapshot in time. It tells us whether a horse appears clinically fit on the date it is evaluated, but it cannot predict every future medical condition or behavioral response.

In my professional opinion, New York City's dense traffic, continuous noise, construction, emergency vehicles, pedestrians, cyclists and other unpredictable environmental stimuli create an inherently challenging and stressful situation for horses. Appropriate veterinary care, thoughtful training and responsible management are all essential for protecting horse welfare, but they cannot eliminate the biological realities of the equine species or the inherent risks associated with asking prey animals to work in one of the world's most complex urban environments. Veterinary medicine can help manage these risks, but it cannot eliminate them. Thank you for your time and consideration.

(03:57:51)

My name is Laura Spellman. I am a founder of the Coalition for Responsible Carriage Horse Retirement and I am here to ask you to vote yes on the Roman Law. I am here as a New Yorker. Mounted police serve a vital public function. Horse-drawn carriages do not.

As an equine specialist, I work with international organizations working with humans and horses. I serve on the Humane Society Large Animal Response Teams. I built and currently run an equine program for the Department of Defense to work with the Navy, the Marines and Wounded Warriors. I work with the VA, the prison system, law enforcement, children with developmental disabilities and the general public. In the last five years I have worked with over 220 horses in my program and I hold a perfect safety record because I put safety first. That means the safety of humans and the horses.

I know that the retirement plan is a central concern here and it can be done. I have done it before. I have worked on a large-scale rescue effort involving over 160 horses, everyone evaluated, treated and placed. I know from direct experience that a coordinated retirement of this very size can be done.

Here is the science as to why they should be retired. A horse's vision spans nearly 340 degrees around its body, but it has blind spots directly in front and behind, so traffic reads as an unseen threat. Its eyes adjust to light far slower than ours, so a shift from sun to shadow or a camera flash causes real disorientation, not disobedience. Their hearing can trigger a full flight response from a siren or a backfire in under a second, faster than any driver can react. Small dogs and wheeled things are allowed in the park and they always will be. This is not a training failure. More training will not fix it. It is biology doing what millions of years of evolution designed it to do, in an environment that will never stop asking it to do the opposite. This is why accidents will not stop. Thank

(03:59:53)

you. Thank you very much. I have a couple of questions for the Conservancy. How much money annually does the Conservancy spend on repairs and maintenance directly related to wear and tear caused by the horse carriages?

I mentioned that because there has been discussion and talk about the cost and financial aspect of a transition.

We are all here as animal protection organizations. However, make no mistake: this is a public safety issue. In 2014, the Animal Legal Defense Fund's successful State Freedom of Information Act lawsuit revealed that New York City's carriage drivers had committed more than a dozen hit and runs over the previous five years, and that cyclists, pedestrians, carriage drivers, passengers had all been physically injured by accidents involving New York City's horse carriages. In one incident, I think this just needs us to go into detail: a child was rushed to the hospital after falling out of a carriage and being run over by a wheel. In another incident, a carriage driver was found by police lying in the street, not moving after being thrown to the ground. Another carriage driver was hospitalized in a coma after his horse became startled and collided with a station wagon.

This latest unimaginable tragedy exemplifies the urgent need to bring an end to the horse carriage industry in New York City. A prohibition on horse-drawn carriages is the only meaningful way to definitively protect the public and the horses in New York City, and we urge you all to pass Int 0943-2026. But I do want to make one last comment: New York City is the greatest city in the world, and it will remain so without horse carriages. Thank you very much.

(04:40:14)

Okay, go ahead. Hi.

(04:40:16)

Hi. Good afternoon. My name is Ayanna Sueme, and I am the Policy and Advocacy Manager at Voters for Animal Rights. We represent a community of over 100,000 New York City voters for whom animal protection is a top priority at the ballot box. First, I want to thank Speaker Menin for not only scheduling this hearing but being an advocate of this Bill. CM Schulman, thank you for your leadership as Chair of the Health Committee. CM Marte, thank you for prime sponsoring Roman's Law. Thank you as well to the Council's Animal Caucus for standing behind this across multiple terms.

New York City voters have consistently supported a horse carriage ban by over a two-thirds majority, and voters are united because the pattern of suffering is undeniable. As previously mentioned, in the 13 months before Roman Schmoe Hodgins' tragic death on June 17 — and thank you to his family for their courage in testifying today — Central Park saw eight separate horse-related incidents. But the casualties of this industry go back decades. The first named carriage horse who died on the streets of New York City was a horse named Mageo in 1982. Since then, dozens of horses have been killed by this industry through sheer exhaustion, traffic collisions, untreated sickness, electrocution, malnourishment and poisonous plants. The causes of death tell the story of the many ways in which horses simply do not belong in the middle of our bustling modern city.

This industry does not just fail horses — it fails the people who work in it, which is why Voters for Animal Rights eagerly supports Mayor Mamdani's Department of Consumer and Worker Protection to transition these carriage drivers into safer, higher-paying jobs, so these workers can not only survive but thrive. The horses deserve better, and the carriage drivers also deserve better than gig work with no sick pay, retirement or health benefits. How many more horses and humans have to die by this industry before this Council decides the cost is too high? Thank you very much.

(04:42:23)

Thank you. Good afternoon, Madam Chair, members of the Council. I am Bill Kizer, Senior Director of Government Relations for the ASPCA, and I am honored to testify in support of Int 0943-2026. The ASPCA has worked to protect horses since our founding in 1866, and our support for this legislation is rooted in that 160-year history.

We have endorsed many efforts to regulate carriage horse welfare over the years. While those reforms have improved certain aspects of the industry, they cannot address the fundamental and persistent challenges associated with horses pulling carriages through one of the busiest, loudest and most densely populated urban environments on the planet. It is inherently dangerous and inherently inhumane. Our position is not based on this summer's horrific events alone. It reflects years of experience and recognition that the risks associated with horse-drawn carriage operations are not avoidable.

Carriage horses must navigate congested streets, construction zones, emergency vehicles, constant traffic and thousands of pedestrians every day. These intelligent and sensitive animals are large and they are powerful, and even the best-trained horse can react unpredictably when frightened or startled. The recent death of carriage horse Denise, followed days later by the tragic death of 18-year-old Roman Mahajan in Central Park, reminds us that the consequences extend beyond equine welfare. These were not isolated incidents. In the last 13 months alone, as others have said, horses collapsed and died, horses have run into moving traffic, collisions have overturned carriages, tourists have been forced to leap from moving carriages and multiple drivers have been injured. This activity cannot be eliminated through additional regulation, and New York City should adopt a lasting solution — not more studies or another decade of incremental reforms. We need a solution that credibly protects horses, supports workers and reflects the values...

(04:44:24)

...of a modern city, and we respectfully urge you to pass Int 0943-2026. Thank you.

(05:46:41)

Good afternoon, Speaker and Chairperson of the Committee. Thank you for the opportunity to testify today. My name is Michael Petrelli. I am an experienced horseman, trained in the field and the founder of Equine Advocacy Alliance. I have spent decades working with horses and I know firsthand the care, respect and understanding they require. My testimony today is based on years of experience with horses and countless hours documenting the realities of the carriage horse industry in New York City.

As a horseman, I can tell you that horses are prey animals. They can be gentle and well trained, but they are also instinctive. When frightened, they react. No law, regulation, inspection or training program can eliminate thousands of years of instinct. That is why horses do not belong in one of the busiest and most congested cities in the world. I have witnessed horses working in crowds. I have documented horses that have collapsed and been involved in carriage accidents. Most recently, just yesterday, two more horses were left unattended by their driver — one taking pictures, no different than what happened to Raymond, and another one who was holding a lead rope and sitting in the carriage in front of him in the back seat, just kicking back and relaxing. I have witnessed horses running loose through Central Park and City streets. Every one of those incidents placed both the horses and the public in danger.

Now we have witnessed the unthinkable. An 18-year-old, Raymond, came to New York City from India to celebrate his graduation with his family. Instead of returning home with happy memories, he lost his life on a horse carriage ride in Central Park. My heart goes out to him and I cannot put it into words. His death is a heartbreaking reminder that this is no longer just an animal welfare issue. It is also a public safety issue.

These are things I learned over three years documenting the horses coming to the park. As a horseman, I expected to be treated and welcomed as one. These people are not horsemen. We have reached the point where the risks outweigh the tradition. We owe it to the horses, to New Yorkers and to every visitor who comes to the City expecting to return home safely. As an experienced horseman, I know what horses need. New York City is certainly not it. It is quite the opposite. As an advocate, I have documented what these horses endure in the streets of New York City. I respectfully ask that you pass Sanchez's law and end the horse-drawn carriage industry in New York City. The time has come to put the welfare of these horses and the safety of the public first.

(06:06:05)

Good afternoon, Chair and members of the Council. Thank you for the opportunity to speak in support of Int 0943-2026. My name is Dr. Jim Came. I am a veterinarian from South Dakota with 38 years of experience as a large animal vet. I am Director of Veterinary Science for the Center for your Main Economy. I practiced, conducted research and taught at three veterinary schools. Four years ago, I came to New York City to evaluate the carriage industry firsthand. I visited the stables, observed the horses that work in Central Park and on the highways, and spoke with both drivers and opponents. My conclusion was clear then and even clearer today: carriage horses should be phased out in Manhattan.

As has been said, horses are prey animals whose instinctive response to perceived danger is to flee. That instinct cannot be trained away or regulated away in one of the world's busiest urban environments, with constant traffic, sirens, construction and crowds. Heat, hard pavement, runaways and serious accidents are not anomalies — they are predictable. Horses evolved to graze on soft ground and live socially in herds, not to pull heavy carriages through congested streets and spend their nights confined alone in small stalls without turnout.

In December 2025, independent equine veterinarian Dr. Craig Kozlowski, who examined horses earlier today, examined more than 12 carriage horses. As you mentioned, they had multiple recurring musculoskeletal injuries, tendon and ligament disease, harness sores and dental disease. He concluded that several horses could not be certified fit for work because they refused standard trotting exams for lameness, and blood work was needed to detect possible pain-masking medications.

Finally, this is also a public safety issue. The tragic death of Roman, together with the numerous carriage accidents over the years that have been mentioned today, demonstrate the risk of placing a frightened, two-thousand-pound prey animal in the

(06:07:57)

middle of Manhattan traffic. For the welfare of these horses and the safety of the public, we urge you to pass this law. Thank you.

(06:08:11)

Thank you for holding this hearing. I am Susan Wagner, President of Equine Advocates, the nonprofit equine protection organization I founded 30 years ago. We operate from a 173-acre horse sanctuary in Chatham, New York, home to 82 horses, ponies, donkeys and mules, which included three former New York City carriage horses — all as a direct result of the cruel and inhumane work they were forced to do and the unnatural lives they were forced to lead pulling carriages in New York City.

The most famous of them was the late Bobby to Freedom, who would have died in a Canadian slaughterhouse if he had not been rescued. Bobby's story proved that New York City carriage horses are sent to slaughter. Bobby was shipped from a slaughter auction. His discharge report stated that he was overloaded with worms, was head-nodding, lame and underweight. Later at Cornell, he was diagnosed with chronic navicular bone disease and fractures from old injuries in both his front legs. Jack, who has been with us since 2016, was diagnosed with navicular disease, evidence of an old bone fracture and bilateral forelimb osteoarthritis. Sheena, who passed in 2023, was anemic, suffered from left limb lameness and hoof ligament disease.

A study by the Coalition to Ban Horse-Drawn Carriages, using information from the Health Department, documented that an average of 71 New York City carriage horses disappeared off the rolls every year over a seven-and-a-half-year period, for a total of 529 horses. Where did they go? We believe: presumed slaughter, presumed dead.

The Mamdani administration says it is progressive — a positive word indicating improvement and moving forward. In keeping with that progressive platform, I urge the new Mayor and the current City Council to allow New York City to finally join the growing number of cities globally and in the United States that have banned horse-drawn carriages. Thank you.

(06:10:16)

My name is Mel Volesky. I am a large animal emergency responder with a decade of experience rescuing horses who have been trapped in fires. I am also a founding member of the Coalition for Responsible Carriage Horse Retirement. I urge you to support Int 0943-2026 and pass Roman's Law.

In my work, the goal is the same: identify the hazard, understand the failure points and act before people or animals are hurt. I am not here evaluating one isolated incident. I am examining a pattern. No reform can make this industry safe because the inherent risk is not a gap in regulation — it is a horse's involuntary flight response. A horse's body is designed for sudden, explosive acceleration. It is the very reason horsepower became the standard metric for engines. When a horse perceives a threat, the fear response fires before the horse's brain has time to process what is happening. This is a reflex. Good training can build a horse's tolerance, but even the best trainers cannot erase survival instincts.

The union's proposed solution is to install hitching posts throughout Central Park. Hitching posts will not remove a horse's flight instinct. They add new layers of risk, creating dangerous conditions on public property that would be approved, installed and owned by the City. If a horse is hard-tied with no chance of breaking away, the restraint itself becomes the danger. This is a known, predictable and documented behavior. A panicked horse that cannot flee will continue to escalate its fear response, increasing the risk of catastrophic injury to anyone within the vicinity. If a horse is tied with a quick-release knot and can break away, it brings us to where we are today, mourning the unnecessary loss of Roman.

Additionally, I would just like to add that horses are the only grazing animal in Central Park, which is why Japanese yew is an issue for them and not for other animals, because a horse is a herbivore. A squirrel or a dog are carnivores. So while this plant may be toxic to species in general, how much they consume and what part of the plant is ingested needs to be taken into consideration, and the toxicology report on Roman's death... well, thank you.

Thank you. You got it. Thank you.

(06:17:30)

Yes, thank you very much. Good afternoon, Chair and members of the Council. My name is Marty Urbi. I am a many-time world champion equestrian, a horseman and past president of two different equine breed registries, and an advocate who has spent many years working to protect American equines through state and federal policy. I have devoted much of my life to this work and ask that you help us see the end of horse-drawn carriages in New York.

Horses are prey animals. They are flight animals. Biology and biomechanics are often overlooked on this issue. When they become frightened, they do not stop and reason through danger — they run. That instinct has allowed horses and other equines to survive for tens of thousands of years. Their natural instincts cannot be trained away. They can only be managed. Yet every day these horses are expected to navigate one of the busiest, loudest and most unpredictable urban environments in the world, surrounded by taxis, buses, construction, emergency sirens, crowds, bicycles and constant traffic. Even the calmest horse can be startled by something beyond anyone's control, and when that happens, the consequences can be devastating for the horse, the driver, the passengers and the public.

This is not about questioning the dedication of carriage drivers — many care deeply for their horses. We have heard that and we have seen that. This is about recognizing that New York City has changed, and our dedication and understanding of animal welfare in this modern era is quite different than it was several hundred years ago. Many other major cities have eliminated the use of horse-drawn carriages and replaced them with more modern transportation and entertainment, like electric carriages. Humans have forced those that were born to run free across open fields to spend their lives standing in traffic. That is not progress. It is a compromise that we no longer have to make.

Freedom applies to more than just humans. We all owe a debt of gratitude to horses and they should be respected, because America was built on their backs. I just want to add: the picture of President Washington up there today on his horse — we do not utilize horses in our military and we do not utilize them in war today. Why should we use them in a major city in this country? Thank you.

Thank you. My name is Clara Bright and I respectfully submit this testimony in opposition to Int 0943-2026 and in support of Int 0970. I respectfully oppose Int 0943-2026 and support Int 0907 because the solution is not elimination — it is improvement.

"A nation that forgets its past has no future," widely attributed to Winston Churchill. Since 1858, horse-drawn carriages have been part of Central Park's history. As the Central Park Conservancy explains, Bridget Law and Olmsted and Calvert Vaux designed the park's carriage drive specifically for scenic travel by horse-drawn carriage. These horses are part of its original design and living heritage. If there are concerns, let us solve them — not eliminate a tradition that has served New York City for nearly 170 years.

If welfare is the goal, continue strengthening oversight, improve infrastructure, install additional hitching posts and pursue the development of modern stables in Central Park North. Some argue these horses would be better off in sanctuaries. However, relocation does not automatically guarantee a better life. These horses have been bred and trained over generations to work alongside humans and receive individualized daily care, specialized nutrition and professional farrier visits every four to five weeks. They need new shoes and regular hoof care — it is called hoof trimming. Not every sanctuary has the resources to provide the same level of individualized daily care that many working carriage horses currently receive.

Regulations are not often followed, monitored or enforced.

Due to a more highly evolved sense of smell, sight and hearing, horses can and do get triggered by sights, sounds and smells which are imperceptible to humans but which signal danger to them. This means they can and do bolt unexpectedly, putting themselves, the driver and any passengers or pedestrians in harm's way. While carriage drivers need and deserve to work like everyone, neither they nor anyone else can count on permanent job security. Industries change and so do consumer preferences. Traditions are created by people and can be changed by people. When a tradition harms an animal or a person, it must end. Today you have heard from many proponents of the carriage horse industry and possibly still you will hear from fellow Long Islander Dr. Kamillah, urging you to trust that this industry can regulate itself. For that trust, the carriage horse industry has repeatedly refused to allow its horses to be examined by independent veterinarians. Instead, it asks this Council to rely on veterinarians like Dr. Sierra, who also serves as the NYPD's veterinarian. Dr. Sierra has been disciplined repeatedly by New York State regulators. The New York State Gaming Commission sanctioned him at least seven times and banned him from the horse track twice in matters involving falsified health certificates, fraud, corruption and administering medication to a racehorse he never examined.

In 2020, the New York State Board for Veterinary Medicine also found him guilty of professional misconduct for failing to properly document the examination and treatment of a horse, resulting in a two-year suspended stay of his veterinary license. When an industry refuses independent veterinary examinations while asking policymakers to rely on a veterinarian with that disciplinary history, transparency is absent and public confidence is impossible.

This law provides a responsible path forward. It phases out horse-drawn carriages, protects carriage horses by prohibiting their sale for slaughter or transfer to another carriage operation, and establishes a workforce development program to help affected workers transition to new employment. I respectfully urge you to pass this law. Thank you.

(07:07:42)

Thank you very much for just hearing me. I am speaking in support of Int 0943-2026. I am a New Yorker, born and raised in Brooklyn, currently in the district. I have been a trauma therapist for 40 years. For 20 of those years I have been an equine-assisted psychotherapist, meaning that I incorporate horses in psychotherapy with clients. Primarily that work has been with combat veterans. In this work I have seen firsthand how horses depend on their senses and their ability to flee perceived danger to stay safe and regulated. We work with a herd of horses at liberty, meaning no halters, no lead lines, no harnesses or any other means of control or domination, and in all these years we have had no injuries to people and no injuries to horses. None.

Horses survive by using their senses to detect danger. Their nearly 360-degree vision, highly sensitive hearing and acute sense of smell are adaptations that allow them to identify potential threats and to react quickly. In Manhattan, those same senses are constantly bombarded by sirens, jackhammers, buses, motorcycles, dense crowds, exhaust fumes, flashing lights and nonstop unpredictable movement. A horse pulling a carriage cannot move freely away from what it perceives as a threat. This conflict between instinct and environment creates an inherent safety risk that no amount of training can eliminate. It is a public safety concern, and protecting the public is one of the City Council's core responsibilities.

Horse-drawn carriages belonged to a different era of New York City's history. Today we know far more about equine behavior and public safety than we did a century ago. We cannot train away millions of years of evolution, and we should not ask horses to ignore instincts that have kept their species alive. The question before you...

(07:09:56)

(07:09:57)

I am almost finished. The question before the City Council is not whether some horses can be trained to tolerate parts of this work, or whether better regulations can reduce the risk to horses and citizens. They walk on hard concrete and asphalt, breathe exhaust, and navigate loud noise and dense crowds, despite their natural instinct to flee perceived danger at a moment's notice. To control them, they wear bits in their mouths, and veterinary research has documented that these bits can cause pain and injuries to the sensitive tissues of their mouth.

Yet horses are still expected to navigate these conditions every day.

Recent tragedies have underscored these concerns. Within a matter of weeks, another carriage horse collapsed and died, and a man lost his life in an incident involving a horse-drawn carriage. These heartbreaking events remind us that the status quo is not without consequences. Cities are beginning to recognize that there is a better way. Just last month, Philadelphia voted to end horse-drawn carriage rides, demonstrating that communities can preserve their history while embracing safer and more humane alternatives.

New York has long been a leader in advancing animal welfare. We have the opportunity to once again lead with compassion by ending an outdated practice that no longer reflects the values of our City. We urge the Council to support Roman's Law, which phases out horse-drawn carriages and prioritizes the safety and well-being of both New Yorkers and the horses who deserve a safer life. Imagine never having a choice, never being able to say I am tired, I am hurt, I am afraid. That is the life of a New York City carriage horse. These beautiful animals were never meant to spend their lives pulling tourists through the busiest city in the world. Horses are meant to run through open fields, graze beside other horses and simply be horses. Instead, they spend their entire lives walking on pavement surrounded by noise and traffic, living a life they never chose nor would ever choose.

Horse-drawn carriages belong to another century. They were created before automobiles existed. New York has always been a city that embraces progress. Today you have the opportunity to let our compassion evolve as well. This is not only about animal welfare. It is also about public safety. Recent tragedies have reminded us of the risk to both horses and people. We should not have to wait for another tragedy before deciding that this outdated practice has to come to an end.

If this were your horse, your dog or the pet you love the most, would you want this life for them? Would you want them breathing exhaust, surrounded by blaring sirens and traffic, walking mile after mile on hard pavement in the heat of summer and the cold of winter? We also heard earlier from their main veterinarian that there is no standard policy in place to ensure the animals' well-being is being maintained. Assessment of the horse's health is left to be determined by the horse's rider or owner, and the horse's owner or rider also decides when to call the vet. How are we ensuring the driver looking after the horse that day will be looking out for the animal's well-being if it means lost income for that day or week?

We also heard earlier how Council Members have yet to be called back to schedule a meeting, and only did so after weeks, which allows time to correct or cover up any deficiencies. Carriage drivers mention horses are creatures of habit, yet there is a pattern of accidents showing that in 30 years horses have yet to get used and accustomed to riding on the streets of New York City and Central Park. In 1982, three horses collapsed and died on the streets, including one in November 1986. A horse took off and crashed into a building in May 1988. A horse fell into a manhole in May 1990. A horse was fatally pinned between a bus and a car in January 2006. A five-year-old horse spooked, bolted into traffic and wrapped itself over the top of a station wagon. The horse broke its leg and died, becoming a major catalyst for an organized campaign to ban New York City carriage horses. In 2014, Spartak, a carriage horse, was pinned to the ground by an overturned carriage after being spooked by a bus. Two years later in January 2016, another horse spooked. I would like to conclude: it is time to ban carriage horses from the streets of New York City and Central Park. This incident only demonstrates an industry that has been overlooked, showing a pattern that has been ignored. Thank you.

Hippotherapy is a medical treatment strategy utilizing the horse's natural rhythmic movement to engage and improve patient sensory, neuromotor and cognitive systems. It is widely used as a modality of treatment for physically and developmentally disabled children. This therapy is also used by veterans who are healing from psychological and emotional trauma.

Carriage horses can successfully transition to hippotherapy or equine-assisted programs. Both therapies require a calm, docile horse with a low flight response. Retired horses are of course evaluated to see if they have the right temperament. They will have the opportunity to be well taken care of and loved. These centers are mostly located at equestrian centers and farm-like facilities, often run by licensed therapists.

My daughter and I have volunteered at different centers here on Long Island, so I have seen the conditions at these facilities. They truly are very wonderful programs.
